L1654: In addition, in the sponsor's analyses of qualitative ECG findings in the AS1 dataset, more subjects on dulaglutide 1.5 mg developed first degree AV block (placebo 0.9%, dulaglutide 0.75 mg-1.8%, dulaglutide 1.5 mg-2.3%). This was also seen in the AS3 dataset (dulaglutide 0.75 mg 1.5%, dulaglutide 1.5 mg- 2.4%).
L1655: Clinically significant AEs under the conduction disorders HLT were balanced (discussed earlier in 7.3.2.9 Cardiac Safety).
L1656: ## Reviewer assessment of ECG interval effects:
L1657: - Dulaglutide does not prolong the QTc interval.
- Small mean increases in PR interval (3-4 msec) were noted with dulaglutide in the placebo controlled studies.
- More subjects on dulaglutide 1.5 mg developed first degree AV block (AS1: placebo 0.9%, dulaglutide 0.75 mg-1.8%, dulaglutide 1.5 mg-2.3%; AS3: dulaglutide 0.75 mg 1.5%, dulaglutide 1.5 mg- 2.4%).
- Clinically significant AEs under the conduction disorders HLT were balanced.
- The clinical significance of these observations would be best seen in elderly patients, patients with underlying ischemic heart disease, sick-sinus or other pre-excitation syndromes, conduction disorders or on baseline beta-blocker/calcium channel blocker therapy. Hence conduction disorders should be studied as an AE of special interest in the ongoing CV outcome study (GBDJ-REWIND).
L1658: ## 7.4.5 Special Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
L1659: Other than the Thorough QT and ABPM studies, no special safety studies were conducted by the Applicant
L1660: ## 7.4.6 Immunogenicity
L1661: See 7.3.2.4 Immunogenicity
L1662: ## 7.5 Other Safety Explorations
L1663: ## 7.5.1 Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
L1664: Definitive dose dependency (i.e. increased adverse events with dulaglutide 1.5 mg vs. 0.75 mg) was observed for the following adverse effects:
L1665: Discontinuations due to adverse events-discussed in
L1666: - 7.3.5 Dropouts and/or Discontinuations
- Nausea and vomiting- discussed in 7.4.1 Common Adverse Events
- Pancreatic enzyme elevations- discussed in 7.3.2.2 Pancreatitis
- Heart rate effects:- discussed in 7.4.3 Vital Signs
L1667: A trend or suggested dose-dependency of adverse effects was observed for:
L1668: - Hypoglycemia- discussed in 7.3.2.8 Hypoglycemia
- Supraventricular arrhythmias –discussed in 7.3.2.9 Cardiac Safety
- PR interval prolongation- discussed in 7.4.4 Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1669: ## Reviewer's Assessment of dose dependency:
L1670: There was a dose dependent increase for elevations in pancreatic enzymes, but the clinical significance of the pancreatic enzyme elevations is unclear since they were not predictive for pancreatitis. All the other AEs with apparent dose dependence listed here have some clinical relevance, except for PR prolongation. The clinical relevance of the observed dose dependent PR prolongation may be better evaluated with the cardiovascular outcomes study.
L1671: Although the dulaglutide 1.5 mg dose has demonstrated superior effects on the primary and secondary efficacy end-points in some trials, I would recommend initiating therapy at the 0.75 mg dose and titrating based on tolerability and need for additional glycemic control based on my concerns around these dose dependent safety signals. Given the uncertainties with some of the safety signals listed above, it seems prudent to avoid a higher dose in patients who do not require additional glycemic control. This can be re-evaluated once the CV outcome trial is complete.
L1672: Additionally, while no significant imbalance in acute renal failure (ARF) was observed and GI side-effects were not worse in the sub-population with eGFR&lt;60ml/min/1.73m2 or macroalbuminuria, the dose-dependency for gastrointestinal side-effects is preserved in this sub-population. Given the post-marketing reports for acute renal failure in this class, it would be prudent to start at 0.75 mg and dose-titrate with close clinical monitoring based on tolerability and need for further glycemic control while use in patients with renal impairment is assessed further. This can be re-evaluated once the results of Study GBDX are available.
L1673: ## 7.5.2 Time Dependency for Adverse Events
L1674: The most relevant AES that were time dependent were nausea and vomiting. These have been discussed in 7.4.1 Common Adverse Events.
L1675: ## 7.5.3 Drug-Demographic Interactions
L1676: For the PT decreased appetite, there was an exaggerated difference between dulaglutide 1.5 mg and 0.75 mg in the ≥ 65 years group (9.6% and 3.2%) compared to the &lt;65 years group (7.3% and 5.5%).
L1677: A treatment interaction with BMI was noted for the PT nausea and vomiting with an exaggerated difference between doses in the lower BMI group (≤ 30 kg/m2).
L1678: ## 7.5.4 Drug-Disease Interactions
L1679: Adverse events in subjects with chronic kidney disease have been discussed in 7.3.2.7 Renal Safety.
L1680: ## 7.5.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L1681: Consistent with class effect, dulaglutide slows gastric emptying and, as a result, may reduce the extent and rate of absorption of orally co-administered medications. This would affect drugs which are dependent on threshold concentrations for efficacy, such as antibiotics, or medications with narrow therapeutic index. In clinical pharmacology studies, the Applicant reports that dulaglutide did not affect the absorption of the tested orally administered medications to any clinically relevant degree and does not recommend any dose adjustment. The clinical pharmacology reviewers agree with the Applicant's assessment.
L1682: ## 7.6 Additional Safety Evaluations
L1683: ## 7.6.1 Human Carcinogenicity
L1684: This has been discussed in:
L1685: - 7.3.2.3 Pancreatic Cancer
- 7.3.2.1 Potential thyroid C-cell proliferation
- 7.3.3.2 Neoplasms
L1686: ## 7.6.2 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L1687: No studies of dulaglutide have been conducted in pregnant or nursing women. There have been seven pregnancies in completed studies in the dulaglutide clinical program. Five of these pregnancies occurred during dulaglutide treatment and two during active comparator treatment (insulin glargine; sitagliptin). Fetal exposure was restricted to the first trimester in all cases. Two of the pregnancies were voluntarily terminated. The other 5 pregnancies resulted in live births. No complications were reported for infants.
L1688: One mother in study GBDD experienced the following maternal complications: mild hypertension, cholestasis, and hyperglycemia. The infant remained hospitalized for 15 days due to low weight, and no other complications were reported.
L1689: One pregnancy (Patient GBDY-327-2703) has been reported in the ongoing Phase 3 studies. Fetal exposure was limited to the first trimester, and no complications were reported for the mother or offspring in this ongoing study.
L1690: ## 7.6.3 Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L1691: No pediatric studies have been conducted to date.
L1692: The Applicant has submitted a request for a partial waiver for pediatric studies in children less than 10 years; and a request for deferral of studies in children with T2DM ages 10 to &lt;18 years until juvenile toxicology studies are completed and the agency concludes that there is sufficient evidence for efficacy and safety in adults. This is consistent with the Division of Metabolism and Endocrinology Product's (DMEP's) approach to other products for the treatment of type 2 diabetes mellitus.
L1693: ## 7.6.4 Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal and Rebound
L1694: The potential for abuse of dulaglutide, resulting in overdose, withdrawal or rebound is low. However, there is concern for off-label use for weight loss.
L1695: There were 23 patients who reported taking more than the designated dose of dulaglutide, including one patient who took 13 consecutive daily doses of 1.5 mg. Events temporally associated with these overdoses were primarily GI events (for example., nausea, vomiting, flatulence), hypoglycemia (reported as non-severe), and small elevations in pancreatic and hepatic analytes (&lt;3x ULN).
L1696: Three of the overdose events were reported as serious using the criterion "other reasons". An SAE (moderate myofascial pain syndrome) was reported approximately 1 month after dulaglutide overdose (1.5 mg dose; assigned dose 0.75 mg; Patient GBCF-029-1511). This event led to study discontinuation

L1698: Not applicable

L1704: ## 9.2 Labeling Recommendations
L1705: Labelling discussions were ongoing at the time this review was completed. It is expected that labelling will be consistent with other GLP-1 agonists.
L1706: Broadly, section 6 and section 14 will have to be revised to include information on the 0.75 mg and 1.5 mg dose. I would also recommend revising section 2 (dosing) to indicate that 0.75 mg qweekly is the recommended dose for initiating therapy with up-titration to 1.5 mg in patients needing additional glycemic control.

L2040: Inclusion Criteria
\ge 18-75 yr of age
T2DM \ge 6 mo
HbA1c (%) \ge 7.0\% and \le 9.5\%
Diet and exercise alone, oral monotherapy, or oral combination therapy
BMI 25 to 40 \text{kg}/\text{m}^2
Study Design Timeline (Treatment Week vs Study Period):
Lead In: Weeks -11 to 0. Background therapy: Metformin (Met).
Treatment Period: Weeks 0 to 52 (Primary Time Point).
Safety Follow-up: Weeks 52 to 108.
Randomization (Week 0):
Dulaglutide + Oral Placebo (Dose selection: 3.0 mg, 2.0 mg, 1.5 mg, 1.0 mg, 0.75 mg, 0.50 mg, 0.25 mg).
Sitagliptin 100 mg once daily + Injectable Placebo.
Decision Point (Week 26): All patients on nonselected arms were discontinued regardless of length of participation in the study.
Switching (Week 52): All patients were switched to the selected arm (Dulaglutide 1.5 mg once weekly + Oral Placebo or Sitagliptin 100 mg once daily + Injectable Placebo).
Legend:
★ Primary Time Point
Randomization
Decision Point: all patients on nonselected arms were discontinued regardless of length of participation in the study
Background therapy
Abbreviations: BMI = body mass index; HbA1c = glycosylated hemoglobin A1c; Met = metformin; T2DM = type 2 diabetes mellitus.
a All treatments were added to metformin \ge 1500 mg/day.

L2042: Figure 4 is a Kaplan-Meier survival curve illustrating the time to rescue therapy (weeks) for patients in the intent-to-treat population across four treatment groups: Placebo, Exenatide, Dulaglutide 0.75 mg, and Dulaglutide 1.5 mg. The Y-axis represents the Percent of Patients Having No Rescue Therapy (%), ranging from 50% to 100%. The X-axis represents Time to Start of Rescue Therapy (weeks), marked at 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, and 12 weeks.
The curve shows that the Placebo group experienced the highest rate of rescue therapy initiation, dropping significantly around week 7. The Dulaglutide 1.5 mg group maintained the highest survival rate (percent of patients having no rescue therapy) throughout the study period, reaching approximately 90% at week 12. The Dulaglutide 0.75 mg group followed closely, and the Exenatide group showed intermediate results.

L2045: Figure 7 displays four plots showing the change from baseline in Clinical Utility Index (CUI) components (HbA1c, DBP, Pulse, and Weight) and the overall Clinical Utility Index (CUI) across various dulaglutide (Dula) doses (0.25 mg, 0.5 mg, 0.75 mg, 1 mg, 1.5 mg, 2 mg, 3 mg) and placebo, compared to sitagliptin. The plots show Bayesian posterior predicted means and 95% credible intervals.
HbA1c (%): Change from baseline is negative (improvement) across all doses, with the largest improvement observed at Dula 1.5 mg.
DBP (mmHg): Change from baseline is negative (reduction) across all doses, with the largest reduction observed at Dula 1.5 mg.
Pulse (bpm): Change from baseline is positive (increase) across all doses, with the largest increase observed at Dula 1.5 mg.
Weight (kg): Change from baseline is negative (reduction) across all doses, with the largest reduction observed at Dula 1.5 mg.
Clinical Utility Index: The overall CUI increases significantly with Dula 1.5 mg, reaching the highest point among all doses shown.

L2056: Figure 15 displays four bar charts comparing the Onset and Prevalence of Specific Nausea Symptoms between Placebo, Dulaglutide 0.75 mg, and Dulaglutide 1.5 mg.
The charts are divided into two sections: Onset of Specific Nausea Symptoms (top row) and Prevalence of Specific Nausea Symptoms (bottom row). The X-axis for all charts represents Time on Treatment (Weeks), ranging from 0-2 to 24-26.
Onset of Specific Nausea Symptoms:
Left Chart (Studies GBCF, GBDA, GBDN Analysis Set 1): Placebo (N=568) and Dulaglutide 0.75 mg and 1.5 mg (N=1670) show similar onset profiles. Nausea onset is highest at 0-2 weeks (Placebo ~12%, Dulaglutide ~12%) and drops significantly thereafter.
Right Chart (Studies GBCF, GBDA, GBDB, GBDC, GBDD, GBDN Analysis Set 3): Placebo (N=568) and Dulaglutide 0.75 mg (N=1671) show similar onset profiles. Nausea onset is highest at 0-2 weeks (Placebo ~8%, Dulaglutide ~8%) and drops significantly thereafter.
Prevalence of Specific Nausea Symptoms:
Left Chart (Studies GBCF, GBDA, GBDN Analysis Set 1): Placebo (N=568) and Dulaglutide 0.75 mg and 1.5 mg (N=1670) show similar prevalence profiles. Prevalence is highest at 0-2 weeks (Placebo ~12%, Dulaglutide ~12%) and drops significantly thereafter.
Right Chart (Studies GBCF, GBDA, GBDB, GBDC, GBDD, GBDN Analysis Set 3): Placebo (N=568) and Dulaglutide 0.75 mg (N=1671) show similar prevalence profiles. Prevalence is highest at 0-2 weeks (Placebo ~8%, Dulaglutide ~8%) and drops significantly thereafter.
